# Section VI. 1) the dispersion-theoretic approach. He also described the standard

quantum theory of multiply periodic systems (Chapters II, III and IV) and its use
1078 Born indeed published a second volume, entitled Elementare Quantenmechanik, together with
Pascual Jordan, several years later (Born and Jordan, 1930). It contained the final version of atomic
mechanics in the form of matrix mechanics.

Epilogue
in one-electron problems (Chapter V), and devoted Chapter VIII to its failure
(The Apparent Non-Conformity of the Quantum Orbits to Classical Dynamics
in Atoms with More than One Electron').
Pauli was allowed—because of the organization of articles on quantum theory
in the Handbuch der Physik—to omit from his review of quantum theory the
discussion of the mathematical methods used in dealing with multiply periodic
quantum systems. He just showed what results these methods had yielded in
special examples of one- and many-electron atoms, and he stressed clearly where
the methods had failed. The topics treated by Pauli and Van Vleck agreed almost
completely: thus both authors wrote sections on the Comp ton effect and the
problems of radiation theory; both concerned themselves in detail with the
theory of line multiplets and the anomalous Zeeman effects; and, above all, both
were not tired of pointing out the importance of the guiding role of the
correspondence principle in atomic theory.
The accounts of Born, Pauli and Van Vleck—especially those of the last
two—reflected the status of the quantum theory of atomic structure in the year
1925 most adequately. The reader could obtain from each report information
about the successes and failures of the theory. None of the three authors tried to
hide the failures. Thus Born concluded his book by referring to the breakdown of
Bohr-Sommerfeld theory in the case of the helium atom and claimed that the old
theory had to be transformed into a 'discontinuous atomic mechanics' in order to
enable the physicists to describe properly the interaction of electrons in many-
electron atoms (Born, 1925, p. 341). Van Vleck made similar remarks at the
corresponding places in his report, while Pauli drew attention to the difficulties
and failure of the old atomic theory already in the case of one-electron atoms.
Pauli, more than the other authors, had contributed to demonstrating the
inconsistency of the Bohr-Sommerfeld quantum theory of atomic structure: he
had calculated the energy states of the hydrogen molecule-ion in his doctoral
thesis in 1921, without being able to reproduce the empirical data; he had
analyzed the anomalous Zeeman effects in 1923 and found that they could not
be described by the standard theory; and he had helped to demonstrate in 1924
that Bohr's theory of the periodic system of elements, the most brilliant result
obtained on the basis of the old principles, was incorrect. Finally, in April 1925,
he had witnessed the experimental disproof of the Bohr-Kramers-Slater theory
of radiation, which had previously been thought to provide the foundation of the
very successful dispersion-theoretic approach of Kramers, Heisenberg, Kronig
and others. Evidently, atomic theory was in a mess, and Pauli expressed his
desperation about its state in a letter to Ralph Kronig in Copenhagen by saying:
'Physics at the moment is again very muddled; in any case, for me it is too
complicated, and I wish I were a film comedian or something of that sort and
had never heard anything about physics. Now I do hope nevertheless that Bohr
will save us with a new idea. I beg him to do so urgently' (Pauli to Kronig, 21
May 1925).
Now Bohr, of whom Pauli expected that he would save physics from its

Epilogue
'muddled' situation in spring 1925 with a brilliant new idea, was in a similarly
desperate mood as Pauli. The main reason was, of course, the fact that the
experiment of Walther Bo the and Hans Geiger had just led to a result which
seemed to destroy the entire scheme of atomic theory set up by Bohr during the
previous two years in order to overcome the failures of the quantum theory of
multiply periodic systems. If these dark prospects turned out to be true, all the
work done on atomic theory since 1913 would lose its value. In a letter to Ernest
Rutherford, his former teacher and fatherly friend, Bohr wrote about 'our present
theoretical troubles which are of an alarming character indeed' (Bohr to
Rutherford, 18 April 1925). Never before had the difficulties appeared so forbidding to
Niels Bohr, and never before had he been left without any glimpse of the
theoretical understanding of the quantum phenomena as in these months of
spring 1925.
When the quantum physicists tried to review in later times the development of
quantum and atomic theory in the first half of the 1920s, they more or less
unanimously characterized the years after 1922 (until the discovery of quantum
mechanics) as years of a deep crisis of the existing theory. For example, Friedrich
Hund in his Geschichte der Quantentheorie, after mentioning the success of Bohr's
theory of the periodic system of elements, stated: 'But shortly afterwards one was
more certain about the difficulties of this older quantum theory, hence in 1922
one spoke about a "crisis'" (Hund, 1967, p. 95). Although the word 'crisis' may
well be used to describe much of the situation in quantum theory in the years
between 1922 and the advent of quantum mechanics in 1925, one does not find
an explicit recognition of such a 'crisis' in the publications and scientific
correspondence of the quantum physicists at that time. Of course, they realized
the failures of their attempts to describe certain properties of atoms and
molecules and stated them clearly. Thus Werner Heisenberg, at the end of his and
Born's unsuccessful helium calculations, remarked: 'What a misery!' ('Es ist ein
Jammer' Heisenberg to Pauli, 19 February 1923). And Pauli, on the failure of his
efforts to explain the anomalous Zeeman effects with the help of the quantum
theory of multiply periodic systems, repeated Heisenberg's words in a letter to
Sommerfeld several months later: 'At the moment, with the theory of the
anomalous Zeeman effects and in general with atoms having more than one
electron, it is such a great misery' (Pauli to Sommerfeld, 19 July 1923). But not
all, or even many, quantum physicists shared Pauli's misery, which lasted from
then until the discovery of quantum mechanics. Heisenberg, on the other hand,
abandoned his pessimistic attitude of spring 1923 as early as fall 1923 after he
discovered a new method, which seemed to overcome Pauli's and his own
difficulties with the description of the anomalous Zeeman effects. This method,
the 'discretization' of the quantum-theoretical equations, filled him and Max
Born in Göttingen with optimism and the firm conviction that it represented an
important step towards the future quantum mechanics. At about the same time
as 'discrete' quantum theory was advocated in Göttingen, Niels Bohr and his
collaborators in Copenhagen gathered new hope, after they had established what

Epilogue
appeared to them to be the correct quantum theory of radiation. Therefore, it is
reasonable to say that neither at Göttingen nor at Copenhagen people felt
something like a permanent crisis in the quantum theory after 1922 for the simple
reason that in the following years they did not stick slavishly to the old theory,
but developed it further by proposing new methods for obtaining more
satisfactory results than before.
To avoid any misunderstanding: none of the physicists mentioned above, and
none of those deeply engaged in the problems of quantum theory,
underestimated the existing difficulties. All knew that many, including some of the most
fundamental aspects of the theory, were still completely unclear. Most believed
that the long march towards a future quantum theory had just begun and that it
would take years, if not decades, until a better, more consistent description of the
known quantum phenomena—and probably of other phenomena which had still
to be discovered—was achieved. Born, for instance, expressed this opinion in the
preface to his Vorlesungen über Atommechanik by calling his book the 'first
volume' of an atomic theory and proposing to write a second volume, which
would then contain 'a higher approximation to the "final" atomic mechanics'; he
added further that the 'projected second volume will perhaps remain unwritten
for several years yet' (Born, 1925, pp. V-VI).
Born was not alone in having this opinion; it was shared by most, especially
the senior quantum physicists. In this context we should remind ourselves of the
fact that in 1925 nearly all physicists, who had ever become involved in quantum
theory, were still alive—perhaps with the single exception of Henri Poincare.
Three generations of quantum physicists were either actively engaged in or
witnessed the developments in the field carefully: the members of the first
generation, which included Hendrik Lorentz, Joseph John Thomson, Joseph
Larmor, Max Planck, Arnold Sommerfeld and Wilhelm Wien, were born around
1860; the members of the second generation, among them Niels Bohr, Max Born,
Paul Ehrenfest, Albert Einstein, and—somewhat younger—Alfred Lande and
Erwin Schrödinger, where born around 1880; and the members of the young
generation, like Wolfgang Pauli, Werner Heisenberg, Paul Dirac, Pascual Jordan
and Enrico Fermi, were born around 1900.
Of the early pioneers of quantum theory only a few did not take an interest in
the recent development. Thus Joseph John Thomson did not make use of the
generally accepted atomic models of the Bohr-Sommerfeld type, but preferred to
construct his own. And Johannes Stark, one of the most ardent propagators of
the quantum concept in atomic theory, retired completely from the pursuit of
new theoretical ideas on atomic structure after 1914. In his opinion, Bohr's
theory not only contradicted important consequences which followed from the
electromagnetic theory, but also disagreed with several observations, e.g., those
on the fine structure of spectral lines (Stark, 1920).1079
1079 We have mentioned earlier Stark's gradual departure from work on the development of
quantum theory after 1912 at several places (see Sections 1.7 and III.4). Stark withdrew more and
more from theoretical considerations and criticized Bohr and Sommerfeld as abandoning the
empirical foundations of physics and turning instead to theoretical and dogmatic points of view.

Epilogue
However, Stark's negative attitude towards the development of quantum and
atomic theory represented an exception. Although several, especially the senior
scientists, by no means agreed with all the new ideas proposed in the course of
time by their predominantly younger colleagues, they believed firmly in the
soundness and correctness of the quantum concept/?er se. Of course, the various
quantum physicists—depending on their own experience of actual involvement in
specific quantum problems—had different expectations about the future
prospects of their field. This difference may be illustrated by comparing the attitudes
of several distinguished physicists.
Among the senior people in the 1920s, Hendrik Lorentz enjoyed a particularly
respected position. Loren tz, who in 1925 celebrated the fiftieth anniversary of his
doctoral thesis on the refraction of light, had been generally accepted as the old
master of twentieth-century theoretical physics. Though, after his fundamental
investigations on the theory of blackbody radiation between 1900 and 1908, he
had rarely concerned himself with the problems of quantum theory—in contrast
he had published several original contributions to another modern theory, the
general relativity theory—Lorentz had not lost contact with the latest
developments. Since 1911 he had acted as president of the Solvay Conferences on
Physics, which at that time dealt primarily with quantum and atomic theory. He
took a special interest, of course, in all questions connected with electron theory;
thus, for example, he studied critically the hypothesis of electron spin, proposed
in October 1925 by George Uhlenbeck and Samuel Goudsmit. That he followed
the current literature on quantum theory is evident from certain statements at the
end of his article on the 25th anniversary of quantum theory, which appeared in
the issue of Naturwissenschaften of 18 December 1925. There he said:
'Admittedly, the fusion of the new ideas [of quantum theory] with classical
mechanics and electrodynamics is still a dream of the future, and we are still far from a
quantum mechanics in whose foundation the discontinuities have been
incorporated. However, promising initial steps have already been taken in this direction'
(Lorentz, 1925a, p. 1082).
Hendrik Lorentz considered the establishment of the correct relation between
the classical and the quantum theory as the most fundamental problem of future
research. This problem bothered him as much as it did Planck. However, unlike
Lorentz, Planck had never ceased to work on the quantum problem himself.
After 1910 he had attempted to remove the quantum discontinuity as much as
possible from the dynamical description by expounding a second and a third
quantum hypothesis (postulating, respectively, continuous absorption by
resonators and continuous absorption and emission mechanisms) and by his theory of
molecular spectra. In the 1920s he had then investigated the statistical behavior
of gas molecules in quantum theory, hoping that it might throw light on the
foundation of the theory. From his long and often laborious work of twenty-five
years, Planck drew the following conclusion: 'The introduction of the quantum
hypothesis does not mean, like the relativity theory, a modification, but a breach
of the classical theory' (Planck, 1926, p. 258). That is, in Planck's opinion, the
quantum theory contradicted the classical theory at many places: on one hand, it

Epilogue
was able to describe phenomena which the classical theory could not account
for; on the other hand, quantum theory appeared to be unable to describe
phenomena like the interference of light which were perfectly accounted for by
classical electrodynamics.
The existence of two completely separate domains of natural phenomena, one
described by the classical, the other by the quantum theory, would not cause any
trouble in case these domains did not overlap. But in reality this was not so. An
example, in which both the quantum and the classical descriptions had to be
applied, was provided by the dispersion of light by atoms and molecules. Planck
regarded the question, as to what extent light could be considered as
electromagnetic waves and how far as consisting of localized quanta, as one of the deepest
quantum-theoretical problems of the future. He considered the proposals,
contained in the papers of the Copenhagen and Göttingen physicists on the
dispersion-theoretic approach, as a step towards the solution of this problem.1080
Planck's positive opinion about the attempts of the theoreticians around Bohr
and Born to formulate an atomic theory based on difference equations was not
shared by Wilhelm Wien, whose blackbody radiation law of 1896 had started the
development leading to the quantum theory. Although he had accepted the
quantum theory and also Bohr's application of it to explain atomic and
molecular structure, he had become increasingly critical of the development of the
theory in the 1920s. In June 1926 he expressed his opinion of it in a letter to
Erwin Schrödinger in the following words: 'The hitherto existing state of the
theory had become so unbearable for me that I had no desire any more to follow
it; not the least did I fear that the younger physicists were not conscious anymore
of what one had to demand of a physical theory' (Wien to Schrödinger, 23 June
1926). In the same letter Wien spoke with contempt about 'the morass of integral
and half-integral quantum discontinuities and the arbitrary use of the classical
theory.' He tended to attribute a part of the responsibility for this unsatisfactory
state of quantum theory to his Munich colleague Arnold Sommerfeld. Sommer-
feld's favourite student Werner Heisenberg was foremost among the authors of
the papers which Wien disliked.
Sommerfeld, who certainly was responsible for the integral quantum
discontinuities, did not feel happy with the Copenhagen-Göttingen dispersion-theoretic
approach either. As we have mentioned in Section V.5, he criticized in particular
the foundation of this approach, which was contained in the Bohr-Kramers-
Slater theory of radiation. After the failure of the quantum theory of multiply
periodic systems, Sommerfeld felt that atomic theory had to obtain a new
foundation. But, unlike Bohr and his associates, he did not believe in the
fundamental importance of the correspondence principle. He rather emphasized
1080Planck expressed this opinion in a lecture, entitled 'Physikalische Gesetzmäßigkeif (*Physical
Regularity9) and presented on 14 February 1926 at Düsseldorf (Planck, 1926). In this lecture he also
referred to the recently discovered quantum mechanics of Heisenberg, Born and Jordan. Although we
do not have a similar public statement made by Planck in 1925 on the situation of atomic theory, i.e.,
before the advent of quantum mechanics, we can assume that Planck thought in a similarly positive
way already in 1925.

Epilogue
the fact that integral quantum numbers had played the decisive role in the
description of spectroscopic data and would do so again in the future quantum
theory.
Most of the physicists, who had achieved a high reputation in quantum theory
during the first quarter of the twentieth century, belonged to the generation born
around the year 1880. We find among them such prominent people as Arthur
Compton, Peter Debye, Paul Epstein, Ralph Fowler, James Franck, Arthur
Haas, Gustav Hertz, George de Hevesy, James Jeans, Edwin Kemble, Max von
Laue, Wilhelm Lenz, Henry Gwyn Jeffreys Moseley, John William Nicholson,
Leonard Ornstein, Fritz Reiche, Owen Willans Richardson, Adalbert Rubino-
wicz, Otto Stern, Richard Tolman, Lars Vegard and William Wilson. What a
wealth of quantum phenomena and theoretical methods to describe them is
connected with the names of these people! But when quantum theory is
mentioned, the names of two physicists must always be recalled: Albert Einstein and
Niels Bohr. It was Einstein who had extended the range of applicability of the
quantum concept beyond blackbody radiation and had established its role in the
description of the photoelectric effect and the specific heats of solids; and it was
Bohr who had opened the door for dealing with atomic and molecular structure
on the basis of quantum theory. In 1925 both Einstein and Bohr had most
distinguished careers as quantum physicists behind them; they were regarded as
the principal authorities in the field. At the same time they had begun to develop
into scientific opponents of each other concerning the deeply fundamental
questions of quantum theory. There were essentially two items which separated
Einstein and Bohr: the problem of the nature of radiation and the role of
probability in the quantum-theoretical description.
Twenty years after introducing the light-quantum Einstein still had to defend
his conception, and he did so in spite of being aware of the fact that the
light-quantum contradicted the electromagnetic theory, whose validity in
describing many optical phenomena he perfectly accepted. Einstein had worked for
many years on the generalized field theory of matter with the goal of uniting the
two theories of radiation; that is, he hoped that this generalized field theory of
matter might explain both the occurrence of light-quanta and the existence of
stationary states in atoms, without contradicting the undulatory characteristics of
radiation in other physical phenomena. In this generalized theory of matter there
should be no place for the concept of probability, which seemed to be necessary
in the quantum theory of the time. The probability concept had entered into
quantum theory from the very beginning in 1900, and Einstein himself had
reemphasized its necessity in 1916 in the description of the processes of emission
and absorption of radiation of light by atoms. If, however, a probabilistic
description could not be avoided in atomic theory, then the concept of causality,
which had been at the cradle of science since birth, seemed to have lost its
meaning. As a consequence, the entire system of existing physical theories was in
danger. Thus, already in 1920 Einstein had written to Max Born: 'The business
about causality causes me a lot of trouble, too. Can the quantum absorption and
emission of light ever be understood in the sense of complete causality require-

Epilogue
ment, or would a statistical residue remain? I must admit that there I lack the
courage of my convictions. But I would be very unhappy to renounce complete
causality' (Einstein to Born, 27 January 1920).
While Einstein in the 1920s was becoming more and more unhappy about
giving up complete causality, Niels Bohr—who had assumed such a conservative
attitude in the case of the light-quantum (see Section V.2)—believed that the
probability concept belonged to the fundamental aspects of quantum theory:
probability was at the heart of the description of atoms and molecules and their
interaction with radiation. Even after the failure of his most audacious proposal
concerning the statistical conservation of energy and momentum in atomic
processes involving radiation, Bohr did not falter in his conviction about the
probabilistic nature of quantum theory. He would enter into serious arguments
with Einstein; the Bohr-Einstein dialogue on classical determinism versus
statistical causality in physical theory would continue until Einstein's death in 1955
without the two opponents reaching any agreement.
Bohr's belief that the probability concept was a necessary element of quantum
theory was shared by two physicists of his generation, Max Born and Erwin
Schrödinger. Both of them had written many papers on quantum theory before
1925, and Born had made the theoretical investigation of the structure of atoms
and molecules the central theme of work at his institute in Göttingen. Born and
Schrödinger had also supported the Bohr-Kramers-Slater theory of radiation
and they regretted its failure. In 1925 they worked on different aspects of
quantum theory: Born tried to extend and deepen the dispersion-theoretic
approach by reformulating systematically the dynamical equations of atomic
mechanics as difference equations (see Section VI. 1); Schrödinger investigated
the problems of quantum statistics (see Section V.5). In this way they tried to
grope their way to a future, better quantum theory.
Among the established members of the community of quantum physicists
belonged, of course, Paul Ehrenfest, born in 1880, and Alfred Lande, who was
eight years younger. Ehrenfest and Lande approached the problems of quantum
theory from very different angles. Ehrenfest was basically interested in the
foundation of the theory, especially its relation to thermodynamics and statistical
mechanics; in 1925 he concerned himself—as we have mentioned in Section V.5
—with the new statistical method of Bose and Einstein, trying to prove (or
disprove) its consistency with the laws of thermodynamics. He was well aware of
the importance of investigating spectroscopic problems, but left them to his
gifted students like Samuel Goudsmit and George Uhlenbeck. Lande, since
many years, had made the quantum-theoretical analysis of spectroscopic data his
central field of investigation. Whenever a detailed model of a many-electron
atom was called for, one which could explain the refined data obtained at
Tubingen's experimental institute, Lande was the first to produce it. He was
aware, of course, of the fundamental difficulties with these models, but he saw
only one way to achieve a better theory of atomic structure: to analyze still more
data and to describe them by more appropriate models.

Epilogue
In 1925 practically all the quantum physicists mentioned in the foregoing
followed the development of quantum theory closely. Most of them treated
particular problems of their own. Some of them, notably Niels Bohr, Max Born
and Erwin Schrödinger, would become central figures in the creation of the new
quantum theory, which came to be called quantum mechanics. However, a major
part of this development would be carried out by younger people, the post-World
War I students of the senior quantum physicists. Many of them, like Wolfgang
Pauli, Werner Heisenberg, Pascual Jordan, Paul Dirac and Enrico Fermi, had
made their appearance on the scene already before 1925. They had all
contributed to at least some aspect of quantum theory, and were well informed about
the status of the existing theory. Only a few among the younger quantum
physicists, however, worried, like Pauli or Heisenberg, about the fundamental
difficulties of quantum and atomic theory. Dirac and Fermi, for example, who
worked very competently on several quantum problems did not at first seem to
be very concerned with the fundamental difficulties of the theory in their papers.
Nevertheless both would make major contributions to quantum mechanics.
The story of the discovery and development of quantum mechanics will be
told in the chapters of the following volumes. In these chapters we will have to
deal in detail with certain problems of the old quantum theory, like the helium
problem or the anomalous Zeeman effects. And we will have to deal with the
educational and scientific backgrounds of the younger quantum physicists, who
became involved in the creation of quantum mechanics, especially Heisenberg,
Jordan and Dirac. In doing so, we shall return occasionally to earlier stages of
the development of quantum theory, to stages which have already been treated in
this volume. We shall, however, try to avoid repetition as much as possible;
whenever we return to a problem that we have discussed before, we shall rather
focus on those aspects of this problem, which were directly connected with the
discovery of the new quantum theory or played an important role in the thinking
of its discoverers. Thus some parts of the following chapters may sound familiar,
but the old problems will appear under a different illumination as steps towards
the resolution of the difficulties in the quantum theory of atomic structure.

given author. The time-ordering letters (a, b, c, . . . ) refer to all the papers of an
author cited in Volumes 1 to 4.
